Wait, I need to be more careful about the implementation. The above approach calculates Grundy numbers recursively, but I need to make sure it's correct and efficient enough for n ≤ 5000.

Let me refine the solution with better handling:

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    n = len(s)
    
    # Memoization dictionary for Grundy numbers of substrings
    memo = {}
    
    def get_grundy(start, end):
        if start > end:
            return 0
            
        if (start, end) in memo:
            return memo[(start, end)]
        
        moves = []
        
        # Try each position in the substring as a cut position
        for i in range(start, end + 1):
            # Check if position i can be cut with some l >= 1
            found_symmetric = False
            for l in range(1, min(i - start + 1, end - i + 1) + 1):
                left_idx = i - l
                right_idx = i + l
                if left_idx >= start and right_idx <= end and s[left_idx] == s[right_idx]:
                    found_symmetric = True
                    break
            
            if found_symmetric:
                # Make cut at position i (0-indexed)
                # Creates three pieces: [start, i-1], [i], [i+1, end]
                g_left = get_grundy(start, i - 1)
                g_right = get_grundy(i + 1, end)
                new_g = g_left ^ 0 ^ g_right  # single character has grundy 0
                moves.append(new_g)
        
        # If no valid moves, it's a losing position
        if not moves:
            memo[(start, end)] = 0
            return 0
        
        # Find mex (minimum excludant)
        move_set = set(moves)
        mex = 0
        while mex in move_set:
            mex += 1
            
        memo[(start, end)] = mex
        return mex
    
    # Calculate initial grundy number
    initial_grundy = get_grundy(0, n - 1)
    
    if initial_grundy == 0:
        print("Second")
    else:
        # Find minimal winning move
        winning_move = -1
        for i in range(1, n + 1):  # 1-indexed position
            # Check if position i can be cut
            valid = False
            for l in range(1, min(i - 1, n - i) + 1):
                left_idx = i - 1 - l
                right_idx = i - 1 + l
                if left_idx >= 0 and right_idx < n and s[left_idx] == s[right_idx]:
                    valid = True
                    break
            
            if valid:
                # Check if this move leads to losing position (grundy 0)
                g_left = get_grundy(0, i - 2)
                g_right = get_grundy(i, n - 1)
                new_g = g_left ^ 0 ^ g_right
                
                if new_g == 0:
                    winning_move = i
                    break
        
        print("First")
        print(winning_move)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Two people play the following string game. Initially the players have got some string s. The players move in turns, the player who cannot make a move loses. 

Before the game began, the string is written on a piece of paper, one letter per cell.

<image> An example of the initial situation at s = "abacaba"

A player's move is the sequence of actions:

  1. The player chooses one of the available pieces of paper with some string written on it. Let's denote it is t. Note that initially, only one piece of paper is available. 
  2. The player chooses in the string t = t1t2... t|t| character in position i (1 ≤ i ≤ |t|) such that for some positive integer l (0 < i - l; i + l ≤ |t|) the following equations hold: ti - 1 = ti + 1, ti - 2 = ti + 2, ..., ti - l = ti + l. 
  3. Player cuts the cell with the chosen character. As a result of the operation, he gets three new pieces of paper, the first one will contain string t1t2... ti - 1, the second one will contain a string consisting of a single character ti, the third one contains string ti + 1ti + 2... t|t|. 

<image> An example of making action (i = 4) with string s = «abacaba»

Your task is to determine the winner provided that both players play optimally well. If the first player wins, find the position of character that is optimal to cut in his first move. If there are multiple positions, print the minimal possible one.

Input

The first line contains string s (1 ≤ |s| ≤ 5000). It is guaranteed that string s only contains lowercase English letters.

Output

If the second player wins, print in the single line "Second" (without the quotes). Otherwise, print in the first line "First" (without the quotes), and in the second line print the minimal possible winning move — integer i (1 ≤ i ≤ |s|).

Examples

Input

abacaba


Output

First
2


Input

abcde


Output

Second

Note

In the first sample the first player has multiple winning moves. But the minimum one is to cut the character in position 2. 

In the second sample the first player has no available moves.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Two players take turns playing a game with a string
2. A move consists of choosing a position `i` in a string where there exists some `l > 0` such that `t[i-l] = t[i+l]`, `t[i-2] = t[i+2]`, etc. (symmetric characters around position i)
3. When a character at position `i` is cut, the string splits into three parts: left part (1 to i-1), middle character (i), and right part (i+1 to end)
4. The player who cannot make a move loses
5. We need to determine the winner with optimal play, and if first player wins, give the minimal winning move

## Key Insights

1. This is a classic impartial game theory problem (like Nim) where we can use Sprague-Grundy theorem
2. Each possible string configuration has a Grundy number (Nimber) that represents its game state
3. A position is losing if Grundy number is 0, winning if Grundy number > 0
4. For each position i in the string, we need to check if it's a valid move (symmetric around i)
5. A valid move exists if there's some l > 0 such that t[i-l] = t[i+l] for all positions from l to min(i-1, len(t)-i)

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Check if any valid move exists initially**
2. **If no valid move exists, second player wins**
3. **If valid moves exist, use dynamic programming with Grundy numbers**
4. **For each position, calculate its Grundy number based on the resulting positions**

Let me implement this:

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    n = len(s)
    
    # Memoization for Grundy numbers of substrings
    # grundy[i][j] = Grundy number of substring s[i:j+1]
    memo = {}
    
    def get_grundy(left, right):
        if left >= right:
            return 0
        
        # If already computed
        if (left, right) in memo:
            return memo[(left, right)]
        
        # Can we make a move at any position in [left, right]?
        # A position i (from left to right) is valid if there exists l > 0 
        # such that for all k from 1 to l: s[i-k] == s[i+k]
        
        valid_moves = []
        
        # Try each position i in [left, right]
        for i in range(left, right + 1):
            # Check if position i can be cut
            can_cut = False
            # Find the maximum l such that i-l >= left and i+l <= right and 
            # s[i-l] == s[i+l] for all valid l
            l = 1
            while i - l >= left and i + l <= right:
                if s[i - l] != s[i + l]:
                    break
                l += 1
            # If we found at least one l > 0, we can cut at position i
            if l > 1:  # l > 1 means we found a valid l (since l starts at 1)
                can_cut = True
                break
        
        if not can_cut:
            # No valid moves, this is losing position
            memo[(left, right)] = 0
            return 0
        
        # Find Grundy numbers for all possible next positions
        next_grundies = set()
        
        for i in range(left, right + 1):
            # Check if we can cut at position i
            can_cut_here = False
            l = 1
            while i - l >= left and i + l <= right:
                if s[i - l] != s[i + l]:
                    break
                l += 1
            if l > 1:  # valid cut at position i
                can_cut_here = True
                
            if can_cut_here:
                # Cutting at position i gives three parts:
                # 1. s[left:i]
                # 2. s[i:i+1] 
                # 3. s[i+1:right+1]
                
                # The resulting game state is the XOR of the Grundy numbers of the three parts
                # But actually, when we cut, we get three independent games, so the Grundy number
                # is XOR of the Grundy numbers of the left part, middle (singleton), and right part
                
                # For a singleton, grundy number is 0 (no moves possible)
                left_grundy = get_grundy(left, i - 1)
                right_grundy = get_grundy(i + 1, right)
                # Middle part (single char) has grundy number 0
                total_grundy = left_grundy ^ 0 ^ right_grundy
                
                next_grundies.add(total_grundy)
        
        # Grundy number is the minimum excludant (mex) of all next grundy numbers
        mex = 0
        while mex in next_grundies:
            mex += 1
        
        memo[(left, right)] = mex
        return mex
    
    # Initial position is the whole string
    initial_grundy = get_grundy(0, n - 1)
    
    if initial_grundy == 0:
        print("Second")
    else:
        # Find the minimal position where first player can make a winning move
        winning_move = float('inf')
        
        for i in range(1, n + 1):  # 1-indexed position
            # Check if position i can be cut (i-1 is 0-indexed)
            can_cut = False
            l = 1
            while i - l - 1 >= 0 and i + l - 1 < n:  # convert to 0-indexed
                if s[i - l - 1] != s[i + l - 1]:
                    break
                l += 1
            if l > 1:  # valid cut at position i
                can_cut = True
                
            if can_cut:
                # Calculate resulting grundy numbers
                left_grundy = get_grundy(0, i - 2)  # 0 to i-2 (0-indexed)
                right_grundy = get_grundy(i, n - 1)  # i to n-1 (0-indexed)
                total_grundy = left_grundy ^ 0 ^ right_grundy
                
                # If this move leads to a losing position for opponent (grundy = 0),
                # then it's a winning move
                if total_grundy == 0:
                    winning_move = min(winning_move, i)
        
        print("First")
        print(winning_move)

solve()
```

Wait, I think there's a logical error in my approach. Let me reconsider.

The key insight is that in impartial games, a position is losing (grundy = 0) if all moves lead to winning positions, and winning if there's at least one move that leads to a losing position.

But actually, a position is winning if there exists a move to a position with grundy = 0.

Let me fix the solution:

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    n = len(s)
    
    # Memoization for Grundy numbers of substrings
    memo = {}
    
    def get_grundy(left, right):
        if left >= right:
            return 0
        
        if (left, right) in memo:
            return memo[(left, right)]
        
        # Find all possible moves from this substring
        possible_moves = []
        
        for i in range(left, right + 1):
            # Check if we can cut at position i (convert to 0-indexed)
            # Position i in substring [left, right] corresponds to index i in 0-indexed
            # Check symmetry around this position
            valid = False
            l = 1
            while i - l >= left and i + l <= right:
                if s[i - l] != s[i + l]:
                    break
                l += 1
            # If we found at least one valid l (l > 1), we can make a move
            if l > 1:
                valid = True
            
            if valid:
                # Make the move at position i
                # Resulting substrings:
                # 1. [left, i-1]
                # 2. [i+1, right]
                # Middle character doesn't contribute to grundy (singleton)
                left_part = get_grundy(left, i - 1)
                right_part = get_grundy(i + 1, right)
                next_grundy = left_part ^ right_part  # XOR of the two resulting games
                possible_moves.append(next_grundy)
        
        # The current position is winning if there's a move to a position with grundy 0
        # Otherwise it's losing (grundy = 0)
        # We need min excludant (mex) of all possible next grundy numbers
        mex = 0
        while mex in possible_moves:
            mex += 1
        
        memo[(left, right)] = mex
        return mex
    
    # Calculate grundy number for the initial string
    initial_grundy = get_grundy(0, n - 1)
    
    if initial_grundy == 0:
        print("Second")
    else:
        # Find the minimal position where first player can move to a losing position (grundy = 0)
        winning_move = float('inf')
        
        for i in range(1, n + 1):  # 1-indexed position
            # Check if we can make a valid cut at position i
            valid = False
            # Convert to 0-indexed: position i corresponds to index i-1
            pos = i - 1
            l = 1
            while pos - l >= 0 and pos + l < n:
                if s[pos - l] != s[pos + l]:
                    break
                l += 1
            if l > 1:
                valid = True
            
            if valid:
                # Calculate resulting grundy numbers when cutting at position i
                left_grundy = get_grundy(0, i - 2)  # [0, i-2] in 0-indexed
                right_grundy = get_grundy(i, n - 1)  # [i, n-1] in 0-indexed
                next_grundy = left_grundy ^ right_grundy
                
                # If this move leads to a losing position for opponent (grundy = 0),
                # then it's a winning move
                if next_grundy == 0:
                    winning_move = min(winning_move, i)
        
        print("First")
        print(winning_move)
```

    n = len(s)
    
    # Memoization for Grundy numbers of substrings
    memo = {}
    
    def get_grundy(left, right):
        if left >= right:
            return 0
        
        if (left, right) in memo:
            return memo[(left, right)]
        
        # Find all possible moves from this substring
        possible_moves = []
        
        for i in range(left, right + 1):
            # Check if we can cut at position i (convert to 0-indexed)
            # Position i in substring [left, right] corresponds to index i in 0-indexed
            # Check symmetry around this position
            valid = False
            l = 1
            while i - l >= left and i + l <= right:
                if s[i - l] != s[i + l]:
                    break
                l += 1
            # If we found at least one valid l (l > 1), we can make a move
            if l > 1:
                valid = True
            
            if valid:
                # Make the move at position i
                # Resulting substrings:
                # 1. [left, i-1]
                # 2. [i+1, right]
                # Middle character doesn't contribute to grundy (singleton)
                left_part = get_grundy(left, i - 1)
                right_part = get_grundy(i + 1, right)
                next_grundy = left_part ^ right_part  # XOR of the two resulting games
                possible_moves.append(next_grundy)
        
        # The current position is winning if there's a move to a position with grundy 0
        # Otherwise it's losing (grundy = 0)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

The mobile application store has a new game called "Subway Roller".

The protagonist of the game Philip is located in one end of the tunnel and wants to get out of the other one. The tunnel is a rectangular field consisting of three rows and n columns. At the beginning of the game the hero is in some cell of the leftmost column. Some number of trains rides towards the hero. Each train consists of two or more neighbouring cells in some row of the field.

All trains are moving from right to left at a speed of two cells per second, and the hero runs from left to right at the speed of one cell per second. For simplicity, the game is implemented so that the hero and the trains move in turns. First, the hero moves one cell to the right, then one square up or down, or stays idle. Then all the trains move twice simultaneously one cell to the left. Thus, in one move, Philip definitely makes a move to the right and can move up or down. If at any point, Philip is in the same cell with a train, he loses. If the train reaches the left column, it continues to move as before, leaving the tunnel.

Your task is to answer the question whether there is a sequence of movements of Philip, such that he would be able to get to the rightmost column.

<image>

Input

Each test contains from one to ten sets of the input data. The first line of the test contains a single integer t (1 ≤ t ≤ 10 for pretests and tests or t = 1 for hacks; see the Notes section for details) — the number of sets.

Then follows the description of t sets of the input data. 

The first line of the description of each set contains two integers n, k (2 ≤ n ≤ 100, 1 ≤ k ≤ 26) — the number of columns on the field and the number of trains. Each of the following three lines contains the sequence of n character, representing the row of the field where the game is on. Philip's initial position is marked as 's', he is in the leftmost column. Each of the k trains is marked by some sequence of identical uppercase letters of the English alphabet, located in one line. Distinct trains are represented by distinct letters. Character '.' represents an empty cell, that is, the cell that doesn't contain either Philip or the trains.

Output

For each set of the input data print on a single line word YES, if it is possible to win the game and word NO otherwise.

........DDDDD...

After that way no train blocks Philip's path, so he can go straight to the end of the tunnel.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 3×n tunnel (3 rows, n columns)
2. Philip starts at the leftmost column (position marked 's')
3. There are k trains, each represented by identical uppercase letters in one row
4. Movement pattern:
   - Philip moves first: moves 1 cell right, then optionally moves up/down or stays
   - Then all trains move 2 cells left simultaneously
5. Philip loses if he occupies the same cell as any train
6. Goal: reach the rightmost column (any row)

## Key Insights

1. Since Philip moves first and trains move after him, we need to simulate the game step by step
2. At each step, Philip can move right and optionally up/down (but not left)
3. Trains move 2 cells left each turn (not 1)
4. We need to check if Philip can reach column n-1 (rightmost column)

## Approach

This is a BFS/DFS state space problem where:
- State = (row, col, turn_count)
- We explore all possible paths from the initial position
- At each step, we try all valid moves (right, right+up, right+down)
- We need to check if the move would collide with any train in the next state

However, since trains move 2 cells left each turn, we need to simulate the train positions over time.

Let me think about the train movement more carefully:
- After Philip's move, trains move 2 cells left
- So if a train is at column j, after Philip's move, it will be at column j-2

## Algorithm

1. Parse the initial grid
2. Find Philip's starting position
3. Use BFS/DFS to explore all possible paths
4. For each state (row, col), try all valid next moves (right, right+up, right+down)
5. Check if the next position is safe (not occupied by any train after the train movement)
6. If we reach the rightmost column, return YES
7. If no path exists, return NO

Wait, I need to consider when a train moves. After Philip's move, trains move 2 cells left. So when we're at position (r, c), the next train positions depend on when the train movement happens.

Actually, let's think about it differently:
- At time t=0: Philip at (r0, 0), trains at their initial positions
- Philip moves to (r', 1) - this is his move
- Then trains move 2 cells left to their new positions
- Philip's next move is from (r', 1) to (r'', 2), etc.

So for a state at column c, the trains' positions are offset by 2c (because they've moved 2 cells per turn for c turns).

When Philip is at column c, the trains have moved 2c cells left, so a train originally at position j will be at position j - 2c.

Therefore, when Philip is at (r, c), we need to check if the current train positions (after their movement) would cause a collision.

But actually, when Philip is at position (r, c), after his move, the trains have moved 2c cells left. So we need to check if at column c, any train occupies the same cell.

Wait, I think I'm overcomplicating. Let me restart:

- Initial state: Philip at (r0, 0)
- Turn 1:
  - Philip moves to (r1, 1) 
  - Then trains move 2 cells left (so trains at col j become col j-2)
- Turn 2:
  - Philip moves from (r1, 1) to (r2, 2)
  - Then trains move 2 more cells left (so trains at col j become col j-4)
- And so on...

So when Philip is at column c, the trains have moved 2c cells left from their original positions.

Therefore, when checking if Philip can move to (r, c), we need to check that at column c, there is no train at position (r, c) after its movement.

A train originally at column j will be at column j - 2c after c moves.

So we need to check that for no train at original position j, we have j - 2c = c (i.e., j = 3c)? No.

Actually, when Philip is at column c, the trains have moved 2c cells left from their original positions. So a train originally at column j will be at column j - 2c.

If Philip tries to move to (r, c), we need to check if there's any train such that j - 2c = c, which means j = 3c.

But this is only for column c. The train at column j should not be at column c when Philip is there.

So for Philip at (r, c), there should be no train such that (original_position of train) - 2c = c, which means original_position = 3c.

Wait, this isn't right. Let me clarify:

If a train originally occupies positions [j1, j1+1, ..., j2] in row r, then after c moves (each moving 2 cells left), it occupies positions [j1-2c, j1-2c+1, ..., j2-2c] in row r.

So when Philip is at (r, c), we need to check that the train's new position doesn't include (r, c).

This means: for any train in row r that originally occupies some interval, after moving 2c cells left, we need c to not be in [j1-2c, j1-2c+1, ..., j2-2c].

This is equivalent to: for any train in row r, if the original interval [j1, j2] contains any position such that j - 2c = c (where j is original column), so j = 3c.

Wait, no: we need c to not be in [j1-2c, j2-2c]. This means c is not in the interval [j1-2c, j2-2c].

This is equivalent to: there is no j in [j1, j2] such that j - 2c = c, which means j = 3c.

So Philip at (r, c) is safe if and only if no train in row r has original position 3c.

But that's not right either. The condition is that c is not in [j1-2c, j2-2c]. This is equivalent to:
c < j1-2c or c > j2-2c
c + 2c < j1 or c + 2c > j2
3c < j1 or 3c > j2

So for a train in row r with original positions from j1 to j2, Philip at (r, c) is safe if 3c < j1 or 3c > j2.

This is the key insight!

So the algorithm:
1. Parse input and get initial grid
2. Find Philip's starting row
3. Use BFS to explore all possible (row, col) states
4. For each state (r, c), try moving to (r, c+1), (r-1, c+1), (r+1, c+1) (with bounds checking)
5. For each next state, check if it's safe (i.e., no train occupies it after the train movement)
6. If safe and c+1 == n-1, return YES
7. Continue until we either find a path or exhaust all possibilities

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    rows = []
    for _ in range(3):
        rows.append(input().strip())
    
    # Find Philip's starting position
    start_row = -1
    for i in range(3):
        if 's' in rows[i]:
            start_row = i
            break
    
    # Extract train positions for each row
    train_positions = [[], [], []]
    for i in range(3):
        row = rows[i]
        # Find all contiguous sequences of the same character (not '.' or 's')
        j = 0
        while j < n:
            if row[j] != '.':
                # Find the end of this train
                start_col = j
                while j < n and row[j] == row[start_col]:
                    j += 1
                # Add the positions of this train
                train_positions[i].extend(list(range(start_col, j)))
            else:
                j += 1
    
    # BFS to find if we can reach rightmost column
    # State: (row, col)
    queue = deque([(start_row, 0)])
    visited = set()
    visited.add((start_row, 0))
    
    # Directions: right, up-right, down-right
    # We can only move to right (same row), up-right (row-1), down-right (row+1)
    directions = [(0, 1), (-1, 1), (1, 1)]
    
    while queue:
        row, col = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we reached the rightmost column
        if col == n - 1:
            return "YES"
        
        # Try all possible moves
        for dr, dc in directions:
            new_row = row + dr
            new_col = col + dc
            
            # Check bounds
            if new_row < 0 or new_row >= 3 or new_col >= n:
                continue
                
            # Check if this state has been visited
            if (new_row, new_col) in visited:
                continue
                
            # Check if moving to (new_row, new_col) is safe
            # After Philip moves to (new_row, new_col), the trains will have moved 2*new_col cells left
            # So a train originally at column j will be at column j - 2*new_col
            
            # Check if any train in new_row occupies new_col after movement
            is_safe = True
            for train_start, train_end in train_positions[new_row]:
                # The train occupies columns [train_start, train_end]
                # After moving 2*new_col left, it occupies [train_start - 2*new_col, train_end - 2*new_col]
                train_start_new = train_start - 2 * new_col
                train_end_new = train_end - 2 * new_col
                
                # If the new position overlaps with Philip's position
                if train_start_new <= new_col <= train_end_new:
                    is_safe = False
                    break
            
            if is_safe:
                visited.add((new_row, new_col))
                queue.append((new_row, new_col))
    
    return "NO"

# Main execution
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

The mobile application store has a new game called "Subway Roller".

The protagonist of the game Philip is located in one end of the tunnel and wants to get out of the other one. The tunnel is a rectangular field consisting of three rows and n columns. At the beginning of the game the hero is in some cell of the leftmost column. Some number of trains rides towards the hero. Each train consists of two or more neighbouring cells in some row of the field.

All trains are moving from right to left at a speed of two cells per second, and the hero runs from left to right at the speed of one cell per second. For simplicity, the game is implemented so that the hero and the trains move in turns. First, the hero moves one cell to the right, then one square up or down, or stays idle. Then all the trains move twice simultaneously one cell to the left. Thus, in one move, Philip definitely makes a move to the right and can move up or down. If at any point, Philip is in the same cell with a train, he loses. If the train reaches the left column, it continues to move as before, leaving the tunnel.

Your task is to answer the question whether there is a sequence of movements of Philip, such that he would be able to get to the rightmost column.

<image>

Input

Each test contains from one to ten sets of the input data. The first line of the test contains a single integer t (1 ≤ t ≤ 10 for pretests and tests or t = 1 for hacks; see the Notes section for details) — the number of sets.

Then follows the description of t sets of the input data. 

The first line of the description of each set contains two integers n, k (2 ≤ n ≤ 100, 1 ≤ k ≤ 26) — the number of columns on the field and the number of trains. Each of the following three lines contains the sequence of n character, representing the row of the field where the game is on. Philip's initial position is marked as 's', he is in the leftmost column. Each of the k trains is marked by some sequence of identical uppercase letters of the English alphabet, located in one line. Distinct trains are represented by distinct letters. Character '.' represents an empty cell, that is, the cell that doesn't contain either Philip or the trains.

Output

For each set of the input data print on a single line word YES, if it is possible to win the game and word NO otherwise.

........DDDDD...

After that way no train blocks Philip's path, so he can go straight to the end of the tunnel.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 3×n tunnel (3 rows, n columns)
2. Philip starts at the leftmost column (position with 's')
3. There are k trains, each represented by identical uppercase letters in consecutive cells
4. Movement pattern:
   - Philip moves first: 1 cell right, then optionally up/down or stay
   - Then all trains move 2 cells left simultaneously
5. Philip loses if he occupies the same cell as any train
6. We need to determine if Philip can reach the rightmost column

## Key Insights

1. Since trains move 2 cells left per turn (after Philip's move), they affect Philip's path significantly
2. Philip's path is constrained by the trains' positions and their movement
3. We can use BFS to explore all possible positions and determine if we can reach the rightmost column
4. A position is safe if it doesn't contain any train that will be at that position after the train movement

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Model the game state
- Each state is a position (row, col) in the 3×n grid
- We need to track which cells are occupied by trains at different times

### Step 2: Understand train movement
- After Philip moves, trains move 2 cells left
- So if a train is at position (r, c), after Philip's move, it will be at (r, c-2)

### Step 3: BFS approach
- Start from the initial position (find the row where 's' is)
- For each position, try all possible moves (right, up, down)
- Check if the next position is safe (no train will be there after movement)
- If we reach the rightmost column (col = n-1), return YES

### Step 4: Safety check
- For a target position (r, c), we need to check if any train will be at this position after Philip's move
- A train at (tr,row, tr,col) will move to (tr,row, tr,col-2)
- So if tr,col-2 = c, then this train will be at position (tr,row, c)

### Step 5: Implementation

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Read the grid
    grid = []
    for _ in range(3):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Find initial position (row, col)
    start_row = -1
    for i in range(3):
        if 's' in grid[i]:
            start_row = i
            break
    
    # Extract train positions - at each row, find consecutive identical letters
    train_positions = {}  # letter -> list of (row, start_col, end_col)
    
    for row in range(3):
        row_str = grid[row]
        i = 0
        while i < n:
            if row_str[i] != '.':
                char = row_str[i]
                start_col = i
                while i < n and row_str[i] == char:
                    i += 1
                end_col = i - 1
                train_positions[char] = (row, start_col, end_col)
    
    # BFS to find if we can reach the rightmost column
    # state: (row, col)
    queue = deque()
    visited = set()
    
    # Start from initial position
    start_col = 0
    queue.append((start_row, start_col))
    visited.add((start_row, start_col))
    
    # Directions: right, up, down
    # Note: Philip moves right first, then optionally up/down
    # But we can only move right in the first step of each turn
    # So we simulate one complete turn at a time
    
    while queue:
        row, col = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we reached the rightmost column
        if col == n - 1:
            return "YES"
        
        # Try all possible moves: right, up, down
        # But after right move, the trains will shift left by 2
        
        # The next move would be to col+1 (right), then optionally up/down
        # But we have to check if the cell at (row, col+1) is safe after trains move
        
        # First, check if we can move right to (row, col+1)
        next_col = col + 1
        
        # Check if (row, next_col) is safe after train movement
        is_safe = True
        
        # Check all trains - for each train, after moving 2 left, will it be at (row, next_col)?
        for char, (train_row, train_start, train_end) in train_positions.items():
            # After moving 2 left, a train at (train_row, c) goes to (train_row, c-2)
            # So if any train's position will be at (row, next_col), then it's unsafe
            for c in range(train_start, train_end + 1):
                new_col = c - 2
                if new_col == next_col and new_col >= 0:
                    # Train at train_row, c will move to train_row, c-2 = row, next_col
                    # So this position is blocked
                    is_safe = False
                    break
            if not is_safe:
                break
        
        if is_safe:
            # We can move right to (row, next_col)
            if next_col < n:
                if (row, next_col) not in visited:
                    visited.add((row, next_col))
                    queue.append((row, next_col))
        
        # Try moving up (to row-1, same col)
        if row > 0:
            new_row = row - 1
            # Check if (new_row, col) is safe after train movement
            is_safe = True
            for char, (train_row, train_start, train_end) in train_positions.items():
                for c in range(train_start, train_end + 1):
                    new_col = c - 2
                    if new_col == col and new_col >= 0:
                        if new_row == train_row:
                            is_safe = False
                            break
                if not is_safe:
                    break
            
            if is_safe:
                if (new_row, col) not in visited:
                    visited.add((new_row, col))
                    queue.append((new_row, col))
        
        # Try moving down (to row+1, same col)
        if row < 2:
            new_row = row + 1
            # Check if (new_row, col) is safe after train movement
            is_safe = True
            for char, (train_row, train_start, train_end) in train_positions.items():
                for c in range(train_start, train_end + 1):
                    new_col = c - 2
                    if new_col == col and new_col >= 0:
                        if new_row == train_row:
                            is_safe = False
                            break
                if not is_safe:
                    break
            
            if is_safe:
                if (new_row, col) not in visited:
                    visited.add((new_row, col))
                    queue.append((new_row, col))
    
    return "NO"

# Main execution
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

Wait, I need to reconsider the problem. The movement pattern is more complex.

Let me re-read: "First, the hero moves one cell to the right, then one square up or down, or stays idle. Then all the trains move twice simultaneously one cell to the left."

This means:
1. Philip moves: right, then optionally up/down or stay
2. Then trains move 2 cells left simultaneously

So in one "turn", Philip makes a complete move (right + optionally up/down), then trains move 2 cells left.

For safety, when Philip moves to a new position (r, c), we need to check if at any point during this turn, there's a train that will be at (r, c) after the trains have moved.

But actually, Philip moves first: he moves to (r, c), and at that moment, there can't be any train at (r, c). Then trains move 2 cells left.

So the safety check should be: when Philip is at (r, c), no train should be at (r, c) at the start of the turn.

But trains move 2 cells left after Philip's move, so after Philip moves to (r, c), the trains at (r, c-2) will move to (r, c).

So we need to check: after Philip moves to (r, c), will any train be at (r, c)? 

A train at (r, c-2) will move to (r, c). 

So we need to check if there's any train in row r that starts at column c-2 and extends to c-2.

Actually, more precisely: a train at row r with consecutive positions from start_col to end_col will move to positions from (start_col-2) to (end_col-2).

So when Philip is at (r, c), we need to check if any train in row r has a segment that covers c-2, because that train would move to c.

The safety condition is: when Philip is at (r, c), there should be no train in row r such that (c-2) is in the range [start_col, end_col] of that train.

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Read the grid
    grid = []
    for _ in range(3):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Find initial position (row, col)
    start_row = -1
    for i in range(3):
        if 's' in grid[i]:
            start_row = i
            break
    
    # Extract train positions - at each row, find consecutive identical letters
    train_positions = {}  # letter -> list of (row, start_col, end_col)
    
    for row in range(3):
        row_str = grid[row]
        i = 0
        while i < n:
            if row_str[i] != '.':
                char = row_str[i]
                start_col = i
                while i < n and row_str[i] == char:
                    i += 1
                end_col = i - 1
                train_positions[char] = (row, start_col, end_col)
    
    # BFS to find if we can reach the rightmost column
    queue = deque()
    visited = set()
    
    # Start from initial position
    start_col = 0
    queue.append((start_row, start_col))
    visited.add((start_row, start_col))
    
    # Directions: after moving right, we can optionally move up or down
    # But in each turn, Philip moves right, then optionally up/down
    
    while queue:
        row, col = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we reached the rightmost column
        if col == n - 1:
            return "YES"
        
        # Try all possible next positions
        # Option 1: move right to (row, col+1)
        new_col = col + 1
        if new_col < n:
            # Check safety: after moving to (row, new_col), will any train be at (row, new_col)?
            # A train in row r will have its segment shifted left by 2
            # So if a train has a segment containing (new_col - 2), then it will be at (row, new_col)
            
            # Check if any train in row row covers (new_col - 2)
            safe = True
            if (row, start_col, end_col) in train_positions.values():
                # Get the train data for this row
                train_info = train_positions.get((row, start_col, end_col), None)
                if train_info is not None:
                    train_row, train_start, train_end = train_info
                    if train_row == row and (new_col - 2) >= train_start and (new_col - 2) <= train_end:
                        safe = False
            
            # We actually have a hash mapping of characters to (row, start, end)
            # So we need to iterate through each train
            for char, (train_row, train_start, train_end) in train_positions.items():
                if train_row == row and (new_col - 2) >= train_start and (new_col - 2) <= train_end:
                    safe = False
                    break
            
            if safe:
                if (row, new_col) not in visited:
                    visited.add((row, new_col))
                    queue.append((row, new_col))
        
        # Option 2: move up to (row-1, col)
        if row > 0:
            new_row = row - 1
            # Check safety for (new_row, col)
            safe = True
            for char, (train_row, train_start, train_end) in train_positions.items():
                if train_row == new_row and (col - 2) >= train_start and (col - 2) <= train_end:
                    safe = False
                    break
            
            if safe:
                if (new_row, col) not in visited:
                    visited.add((new_row, col))
                    queue.append((new_row, col))
        
        # Option 3: move down to (row+1, col)
        if row < 2:
            new_row = row + 1
            # Check safety for (new_row, col)
            safe = True
            for char, (train_row, train_start, train_end) in train_positions.items():
                if train_row == new_row and (col - 2) >= train_start and (col - 2) <= train_end:
                    safe = False
                    break
            
            if safe:
                if (new_row, col) not in visited:
                    visited.add((new_row, col))
                    queue.append((new_row, col))
    
    return "NO"

# Main execution
```

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Read the grid
    grid = []
    for _ in range(3):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Find initial position (row, col)

    for i in range(3):
        if 's' in grid[i]:
            start_row = i
            break
    
    # Extract train positions - at each row, find consecutive identical letters
    train_positions = {}  # char -> (row, start_col, end_col)
    
    for row in range(3):
        row_str = grid[row]
        i = 0
        while i < n:
            if row_str[i] != '.':
                char = row_str[i]
                start_col = i
                while i < n and row_str[i] == char:
                    i += 1
                end_col = i - 1
                train_positions[char] = (row, start_col, end_col)
    
    # BFS to find if we can reach the rightmost column
    queue = deque()
    visited = set()
    
    # Start from initial position
    start_col = 0
    queue.append((start_row, start_col))
    visited.add((start_row, start_col))
    
    while queue:
        row, col = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we reached the rightmost column
        if col == n - 1:
            return "YES"
        
        # Try all possible next positions
        
        # Option 1: move right to (row, col+1)
        new_col = col + 1
        if new_col < n:
            # Check safety: after moving to (row, new_col), will any train be at (row, new_col)?
            # A train in row r will have its segment shifted left by 2
            # So if a train has a segment containing (new_col - 2), then it will be at (row, new_col)
            
            safe = True
            for char, (train_row, train_start, train_end) in train_positions.items():
                if train_row == row and (new_col - 2) >= train_start and (new_col - 2) <= train_end:
                    safe = False
                    break
            
            if safe:
                if (row, new_col) not in visited:
                    visited.add((row, new_col))
                    queue.append((row, new_col))
        
        # Option 2: move up to (row-1, col)
        if row > 0:
            new_row = row - 1
            # Check safety for (new_row, col)
            safe = True
            for char, (train_row, train_start, train_end) in train_positions.items():
                if train_row == new_row and (col - 2) >= train_start and (col - 2) <= train_end:
                    safe = False
                    break
            
            if safe:
                if (new_row, col) not in visited:
                    visited.add((new_row, col))
                    queue.append((new_row, col))
        
        # Option 3: move down to (row+1, col)
        if row < 2:
            new_row = row + 1
            # Check safety for (new_row, col)
            safe = True
            for char, (train_row, train_start, train_end) in train_positions.items():
                if train_row == new_row and (col - 2) >= train_start and (col - 2) <= train_end:
                    safe = False
                    break
            
            if safe:
                if (new_row, col) not in visited:
                    visited.add((new_row, col))
                    queue.append((new_row, col))
    
    return "NO"

# Main execution
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Read the grid
    grid = []
    for _ in range(3):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Find initial position (row, col)
    start_row = -1
    for i in range(3):
        if 's' in grid[i]:
            start_row = i
            break
    
    # Extract train positions - at each row, find consecutive identical letters
    train_positions = {}  # char -> (row, start_col, end_col)
    
    for row in range(3):
        row_str = grid[row]
        i = 0
        while i < n:
            if row_str[i] != '.':
                char = row_str[i]
                start_col = i
                while i < n and row_str[i] == char:
                    i += 1
                end_col = i - 1
                train_positions[char] = (row, start_col, end_col)
    
    # BFS to find if we can reach the rightmost column
    queue = deque()
    visited = set()
    
    # Start from initial position
    start_col = 0
    queue.append((start_row, start_col))
    visited.add((start_row, start_col))
    
    while queue:
        row, col = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we reached the rightmost column
        if col == n - 1:

        # Try all possible next positions
        
        # Option 1: move right to (row, col+1)
        new_col = col + 1
        if new_col < n:
            # Check safety: after moving to (row, new_col), will any train be at (row, new_col)?
            # A train in row r will have its segment shifted left by 2
            # So if a train has a segment containing (new_col - 2), then it will be at (row, new_col)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

The mobile application store has a new game called "Subway Roller".

The protagonist of the game Philip is located in one end of the tunnel and wants to get out of the other one. The tunnel is a rectangular field consisting of three rows and n columns. At the beginning of the game the hero is in some cell of the leftmost column. Some number of trains rides towards the hero. Each train consists of two or more neighbouring cells in some row of the field.

All trains are moving from right to left at a speed of two cells per second, and the hero runs from left to right at the speed of one cell per second. For simplicity, the game is implemented so that the hero and the trains move in turns. First, the hero moves one cell to the right, then one square up or down, or stays idle. Then all the trains move twice simultaneously one cell to the left. Thus, in one move, Philip definitely makes a move to the right and can move up or down. If at any point, Philip is in the same cell with a train, he loses. If the train reaches the left column, it continues to move as before, leaving the tunnel.

Your task is to answer the question whether there is a sequence of movements of Philip, such that he would be able to get to the rightmost column.

<image>

Input

Each test contains from one to ten sets of the input data. The first line of the test contains a single integer t (1 ≤ t ≤ 10 for pretests and tests or t = 1 for hacks; see the Notes section for details) — the number of sets.

Then follows the description of t sets of the input data. 

The first line of the description of each set contains two integers n, k (2 ≤ n ≤ 100, 1 ≤ k ≤ 26) — the number of columns on the field and the number of trains. Each of the following three lines contains the sequence of n character, representing the row of the field where the game is on. Philip's initial position is marked as 's', he is in the leftmost column. Each of the k trains is marked by some sequence of identical uppercase letters of the English alphabet, located in one line. Distinct trains are represented by distinct letters. Character '.' represents an empty cell, that is, the cell that doesn't contain either Philip or the trains.

Output

For each set of the input data print on a single line word YES, if it is possible to win the game and word NO otherwise.

Examples

16 4
...AAAAA........
s.BBB......CCCCC
........DDDDD...

...AAAAA........
s.BBB....CCCCC..
.......DDDDD....


Output

YES
NO

s.ZZ......

.YYYYYY...

After that way no train blocks Philip's path, so he can go straight to the end of the tunnel.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 3-row by n-column tunnel
2. Philip starts at the leftmost column (first column) with position 's'
3. There are k trains, each represented by identical uppercase letters in consecutive cells in one row

## Key Insights

1. Since trains move 2 cells left per turn (after Philip's move), they can "escape" to the left boundary when they reach column 1
2. Philip moves one cell right per turn, plus optional vertical movement
3. The critical observation is that trains move in groups and their positions change predictably

## Strategy

## Solution Approach

Use BFS with state (row, col, time) or just (row, col) since time can be derived from col (time = col, since Philip moves one cell right per turn).

Actually, time is equal to the column number, because Philip starts at column 0 at time 0, moves to column 1 at time 1, etc.

Therefore, when Philip is at column c, the time is c.

So for a train segment in row r, originally occupying [s, s+L-1], at time c, it occupies [s-2c, s-2c+L-1].

Philip at (r, c) is safe if there's no train segment in row r such that c is in [s-2c, s-2c+L-1].

For each such segment, we have:
s-2c <= c <= s-2c+L-1

Simplifying:
s-2c <= c → s <= 3c
c <= s-2c+L-1 → 3c <= s+L-1

So the condition for safety is that for no train segment (s, L) in row r, we have s <= 3c and 3c <= s+L-1.

We can precompute for each row the train segments.

## Algorithm

1. Parse input to extract train segments for each row
2. Use BFS starting from the initial position (r, 0)
3. For each state (r, c), we can move to:
   - (r, c+1) - move right
   - (r-1, c+1) - move up (if r-1 >= 0)
   - (r+1, c+1) - move down (if r+1 < 3)
4. At each new column c, calculate time = c
5. Check if the new position (r, c) is safe by verifying for each train segment in row r:
   - s-2c <= c <= s-2c+L-1
   - This is equivalent to s <= 3c and 3c <= s+L-1
6. If safe, add to queue
7. If we reach any column c >= n-1, return YES
8. If BFS completes without reaching end, return NO

Wait, we want to reach the rightmost column, which is column n-1.

## Implementation

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    rows = []
    for _ in range(3):
        rows.append(input().strip())
    
    # Parse train segments in each row
    train_segments = []
    for i in range(3):
        row = rows[i]
        segments = []
        j = 0
        while j < n:
            if row[j] == '.':
                j += 1
            else:
                # Found a train segment
                char = row[j]
                start = j
                while j < n and row[j] == char:
                    j += 1
                length = j - start
                segments.append((start, length))
        train_segments.append(segments)
    
    # BFS from initial position
    # Find initial row
    start_row = -1
    for i in range(3):
        if 's' in rows[i]:
            start_row = i
            break
    
    # BFS: (row, col)
    visited = set()
    queue = deque([(start_row, 0)])
    visited.add((start_row, 0))
    
    # We want to reach column n-1
    while queue:
        row, col = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we reached the rightmost column
        if col == n - 1:
            return "YES"
            
        # Try all possible next moves: right, up, down
        next_col = col + 1
        
        # Move right
        next_state = (row, next_col)
        if next_col < n:  # Don't go beyond rightmost column
            # Check if safe at time = next_col (since time = column)
            safe = True
            for start, length in train_segments[row]:
                # Train occupies [start - 2*next_col, start - 2*next_col + length - 1] at time next_col
                train_start = start - 2 * next_col
                train_end = start - 2 * next_col + length - 1
                
                # Check if current position (row, next_col) is in this train segment
                if train_start <= next_col <= train_end:
                    safe = False
                    break
                    
            if safe and next_state not in visited:
                visited.add(next_state)
                queue.append(next_state)
        
        # Move up
        if row > 0:
            next_state = (row - 1, next_col)
            if next_col < n:
                safe = True
                for start, length in train_segments[row - 1]:
                    train_start = start - 2 * next_col
                    train_end = start - 2 * next_col + length - 1
                    if train_start <= next_col <= train_end:
                        safe = False
                        break
                if safe and next_state not in visited:
                    visited.add(next_state)
                    queue.append(next_state)
        
        # Move down
        if row < 2:
            next_state = (row + 1, next_col)
            if next_col < n:
                safe = True
                for start, length in train_segments[row + 1]:
                    train_start = start - 2 * next_col
                    train_end = start - 2 * next_col + length - 1
                    if train_start <= next_col <= train_end:
                        safe = False
                        break
                if safe and next_state not in visited:
                    visited.add(next_state)
                    queue.append(next_state)
    
    return "NO"

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

1. Philip moves first: one cell right, then optionally up/down or stay
2. Then trains move 2 cells left

So the sequence is:
- At time 0: Philip at (r,0)
- After Philip's move: Philip at (r',1) or (r',2) or (r,1)
- Then trains move left by 2 cells

So when Philip is at column 1, the time is 1, and trains have moved 2 cells left.

In my BFS, when I'm at (r, c), the time is c, and the trains have moved 2c cells left.

But when I generate the next state, I'm moving to column c+1, which happens after Philip's move at time c, then trains move 2c cells left.

The safety check should be at time c (when Philip is at column c), but the trains have already moved.

I need to be more careful.

Actually, let me reframe:

- At time 0: Philip starts at (start_row, 0)
- At time 1: After Philip moves to (r',1), then trains move 2 cells left
- So at time 1, trains are at positions reduced by 2

So when Philip is at column c, the time is c, and trains have moved 2c cells left.

So for a train that originally started at position s, at time c it occupies [s-2c, s-2c+L-1].

Philip at (r, c) collides with a train if c is in [s-2c, s-2c+L-1].

Yes, this is what I have.

But let me verify with the first example:
- n=16, k=4
- Row 0: "...AAAAA........"
- Row 1: "s.BBB......CCCCC"
- Row 2: "........DDDDD..."

At time 1, Philip moves to row r, column 1. Trains have moved 2 cells left.

First, the hero moves one cell to the right, then one square up or down, or stays idle. Then all the trains move twice simultaneously one cell to the left."

"move twice simultaneously one cell to the left" - this means they move 2 cells to the left in one turn.

Original C at 9,10,11,12 → at 5,6,7,8

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    rows = []
    for _ in range(3):
        rows.append(input().strip())
    
    # Parse train segments in each row
    train_segments = []
    for i in range(3):
        row = rows[i]
        segments = []
        j = 0
        while j < n:
            if row[j] == '.':
                j += 1
            else:
                # Found a train segment
                char = row[j]
                start = j
                while j < n and row[j] == char:
                    j += 1
                length = j - start
                segments.append((start, length))
        train_segments.append(segments)
    
    # Find initial row
    start_row = -1
    for i in range(3):
        if 's' in rows[i]:
            start_row = i
            break
    
    # BFS: (row, col)
    visited = set()
    queue = deque([(start_row, 0)])
    visited.add((start_row, 0))
    
    while queue:
        row, col = queue.popleft()
        
        # If we reached the rightmost column
        if col == n - 1:
            return "YES"
            
        # Try all next moves: right, up, down
        next_col = col + 1
        
        # Move right
        next_state = (row, next_col)
        if next_col < n:  # within bounds
            # Check safety at time = next_col
            safe = True
            for start, length in train_segments[row]:
                # Train occupies [start - 2*next_col, start - 2*next_col + length - 1] at time next_col
                train_start = start - 2 * next_col
                train_end = start - 2 * next_col + length - 1
                
                # Check if next_state (row, next_col) is in this train segment
                if train_start <= next_col <= train_end:
                    safe = False
                    break
                    
            if safe and next_state not in visited:
                visited.add(next_state)
                queue.append(next_state)
        
        # Move up
        if row > 0:
            next_state = (row - 1, next_col)
            if next_col < n:
                safe = True
                for start, length in train_segments[row - 1]:
                    train_start = start - 2 * next_col
                    train_end = start - 2 * next_col + length - 1
                    if train_start <= next_col <= train_end:
                        safe = False
                        break
                if safe and next_state not in visited:
                    visited.add(next_state)
                    queue.append(next_state)
        
        # Move down
        if row < 2:
            next_state = (row + 1, next_col)
            if next_col < n:
                safe = True
                for start, length in train_segments[row + 1]:
                    train_start = start - 2 * next_col
                    train_end = start - 2 * next_col + length - 1
                    if train_start <= next_col <= train_end:
                        safe = False
                        break
                if safe and next_state not in visited:
                    visited.add(next_state)
                    queue.append(next_state)
    
    return "NO"

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

The mobile application store has a new game called "Subway Roller".

The protagonist of the game Philip is located in one end of the tunnel and wants to get out of the other one. The tunnel is a rectangular field consisting of three rows and n columns. At the beginning of the game the hero is in some cell of the leftmost column. Some number of trains rides towards the hero. Each train consists of two or more neighbouring cells in some row of the field.

All trains are moving from right to left at a speed of two cells per second, and the hero runs from left to right at the speed of one cell per second. For simplicity, the game is implemented so that the hero and the trains move in turns. First, the hero moves one cell to the right, then one square up or down, or stays idle. Then all the trains move twice simultaneously one cell to the left. Thus, in one move, Philip definitely makes a move to the right and can move up or down. If at any point, Philip is in the same cell with a train, he loses. If the train reaches the left column, it continues to move as before, leaving the tunnel.

Your task is to answer the question whether there is a sequence of movements of Philip, such that he would be able to get to the rightmost column.

<image>

Input

Each test contains from one to ten sets of the input data. The first line of the test contains a single integer t (1 ≤ t ≤ 10 for pretests and tests or t = 1 for hacks; see the Notes section for details) — the number of sets.

Then follows the description of t sets of the input data. 

The first line of the description of each set contains two integers n, k (2 ≤ n ≤ 100, 1 ≤ k ≤ 26) — the number of columns on the field and the number of trains. Each of the following three lines contains the sequence of n character, representing the row of the field where the game is on. Philip's initial position is marked as 's', he is in the leftmost column. Each of the k trains is marked by some sequence of identical uppercase letters of the English alphabet, located in one line. Distinct trains are represented by distinct letters. Character '.' represents an empty cell, that is, the cell that doesn't contain either Philip or the trains.

Output

For each set of the input data print on a single line word YES, if it is possible to win the game and word NO otherwise.

Examples

Input

2
16 4
...AAAAA........
s.BBB......CCCCC
........DDDDD...
16 4
...AAAAA........
s.BBB....CCCCC..
.......DDDDD....


Output

YES
NO


Input

2
10 4
s.ZZ......
.....AAABB
.YYYYYY...
10 4
s.ZZ......
....AAAABB
.YYYYYY...


Output

YES
NO

In the first set of the input of the first sample Philip must first go forward and go down to the third row of the field, then go only forward, then go forward and climb to the second row, go forward again and go up to the first row. After that way no train blocks Philip's path, so he can go straight to the end of the tunnel.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 3×n tunnel (3 rows, n columns)
2. Philip starts at the leftmost column (position 's')
3. There are k trains, each represented by identical uppercase letters in a row
4. Movement pattern:
   - Philip moves first: 1 cell right, then optionally up/down or stays idle
   - Then all trains move 2 cells left simultaneously
5. If Philip occupies the same cell as any train at any time, he loses
6. Goal: Reach the rightmost column (last column) safely

## Key Insights

1. Philip moves 1 cell right in each turn, then can move up/down or stay
2. Trains move 2 cells left in each turn
3. The game progresses turn by turn
4. We need to find if there's a sequence of moves that gets Philip to the rightmost column

## Approach

This is a BFS/DFS problem where we explore all possible paths Philip can take.

For each state, we need to track:
- Philip's current position (row, col)
- The current positions of all trains (or rather, their "current" state after moves)

However, since trains move predictably (2 cells left per turn), we can simulate the game turn by turn.

Key observations:
- Philip's x-coordinate increases by 1 each turn
- Trains' x-coordinates decrease by 2 each turn
- We need to check if Philip can reach column n-1 (rightmost column)
- At each turn, after Philip moves, we check if he collides with any train

## Algorithm

1. Parse input to get initial positions
2. Use BFS/DFS to explore all possible paths
3. Each state is (row, col, turn) where turn represents how many turns have passed
4. For each state, try all possible vertical moves (up, down, stay)
5. After Philip moves, check if he collides with any train
6. If no collision and Philip hasn't reached the rightmost column, continue
7. If Philip reaches the rightmost column, return YES

But we need to track train positions over time. Since trains move 2 cells left per turn, at turn t, a train initially at column c will be at column (c - 2*t).

However, since trains start at column 0 or later, and we only care about positions where Philip is, we need to check collisions at each step.

Wait, let me reconsider. The trains move 2 cells left each turn, but only when they move. So at time t (after t turns), a train that started at column c will be at column (c - 2*t).

But since the tunnel has only n columns, a train might go out of bounds (left of column 0). When it goes out of bounds, it disappears and doesn't block any more.

So for each position of Philip at turn t, we need to check if any train at the same row and column (with column adjusted by -2*t) is present.

## Implementation Steps

1. Parse input to get initial grid and train locations
2. For each train letter, store its initial positions (row, column)
3. Use BFS to explore possible paths
4. State: (row, col, turn) 
5. From each state, Philip moves right (col + 1), then up/down/stay
6. After Philip's move, calculate where each train is (initial_col - 2 * turn)
7. Check if Philip collides with any train at the new position
8. If no collision and col + 1 == n, we win
9. Use a visited set to avoid cycles

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(3):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Find initial position of Philip and train positions
    train_positions = {}
    philip_row, philip_col = -1, -1
    
    for i in range(3):
        for j in range(n):
            if grid[i][j] == 's':
                philip_row, philip_col = i, j
            elif grid[i][j] != '.':
                if grid[i][j] not in train_positions:
                    train_positions[grid[i][j]] = []
                train_positions[grid[i][j]].append(j)
    
    # BFS to find if Philip can reach the end
    # State: (row, col, turn) - turn is the number of turns passed
    queue = deque([(philip_row, philip_col, 0)])
    visited = set()
    visited.add((philip_row, philip_col, 0))
    
    # Directions: up, down, stay (we don't need to move vertically)
    # Actually, after moving right, Philip can move up, down, or stay
    # So we have three choices: stay, move up, move down
    
    while queue:
        row, col, turn = queue.popleft()
        
        # Try all possible vertical movements after moving right
        # First move right to col+1
        new_col = col + 1
        
        # If we've reached the rightmost column, we win
        if new_col >= n:
            return "YES"
        
        # Try vertical moves: up, down, stay
        for dr in [-1, 0, 1]:  # up, stay, down
            new_row = row + dr
            
            # Check if new position is within bounds
            if new_row < 0 or new_row >= 3:
                continue
                
            # Check collision with trains at this turn
            collision = False
            for train_char, positions in train_positions.items():
                # For each train, calculate its current column after 'turn' turns
                # Train moves 2 cells left per turn, so current column = initial_col - 2 * turn
                for pos in positions:
                    current_col = pos - 2 * turn
                    # If current_col < 0, train is out of bounds
                    if current_col >= 0 and current_col == new_col and new_row == row:
                        # We need to check if this train is actually present at (new_row, new_col)
                        # But wait, the train is only present at its specific row
                        # So if train_char is at row new_row and column current_col, then collision
                        if new_row == row:  # This is wrong, train is in a specific row
                            # Actually, each train is in a specific row
                            # So we need to check if the train in row new_row exists at column current_col
                            pass
            
            # Actually, let me fix this: trains are in specific rows
            # For a train in row r with initial position c, after turn turns, it's at column c - 2*turn
            # So at turn t, in row r, column (c - 2*t), there's a train if c - 2*t >= 0
            
            collision = False
            for train_char, positions in train_positions.items():
                # Each train has a specific row? Actually, in input, trains are given in their row
                # But we don't have row information stored separately yet
                
                # Let me restructure: we need to know for each train track (row, letter)
                pass
    
    # Let me restart with a better structure
    
    # Parse train positions by row
    train_by_row = {}
    for i in range(3):
        row_train = []
        for j in range(n):
            if grid[i][j] != '.' and grid[i][j] != 's':
                char = grid[i][j]
                if char not in train_by_row:
                    train_by_row[char] = []
                train_by_row[char].append((i, j))
    
    # BFS with state (row, col, turn)
    queue = deque([(philip_row, philip_col, 0)])
    visited = set()
    visited.add((philip_row, philip_col, 0))
    
    while queue:
        row, col, turn = queue.popleft()
        
        # Move right to col+1
        new_col = col + 1
        
        # If reached rightmost column
        if new_col >= n:
            return "YES"
            
        # Try vertical movements: up, down, stay
        for dr in [-1, 0, 1]:
            new_row = row + dr
            if new_row < 0 or new_row >= 3:
                continue
                
            # Check collision with trains at this state
            collision = False
            for train_char, positions in train_by_row.items():
                for train_row, train_col in positions:
                    # In turn 'turn', this train moves 2 cells left
                    current_col = train_col - 2 * turn
                    # If current_col >= 0, the train is still in the tunnel
                    if current_col >= 0 and current_col == new_col and train_row == new_row:
                        collision = True
                        break
                if collision:
                    break
                    
            if not collision:
                # New state is (new_row, new_col, turn+1)
                state = (new_row, new_col, turn + 1)
                if state not in visited:
                    visited.add(state)
                    queue.append(state)
                    
    return "NO"

t = int(input())
```

```python
def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(3):
```

Wait, I see the issue - I'm storing train positions in a structure that doesn't separate by row properly. Let me fix:

```python
from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(3):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Store trains by row: row -> {char: list of column positions}
    train_rows = [dict() for _ in range(3)]
    
    # Find Philip's initial position
    philip_row, philip_col = 0, 0
    
    for i in range(3):
        for j in range(n):
            if grid[i][j] == 's':
                philip_row, philip_col = i, j
            elif grid[i][j] != '.':
                char = grid[i][j]
                if char not in train_rows[i]:
                    train_rows[i][char] = []
                train_rows[i][char].append(j)
    
    # BFS: (row, col, turn)
    queue = deque([(philip_row, philip_col, 0)])
    visited = set()
    visited.add((philip_row, philip_col, 0))
    
    # Directions: up, down, stay
    while queue:
        row, col, turn = queue.popleft()
        
        # Move right to col+1
        new_col = col + 1
        
        # If we've reached the rightmost column
        if new_col >= n:
            return "YES"
            
        # Try vertical moves: up, down, stay
        for dr in [-1, 0, 1]:
            new_row = row + dr
            if new_row < 0 or new_row >= 3:
                continue
                
            # Check collision with trains after 'turn' turns
            collision = False
            for train_row in range(3):
                if train_row == new_row:  # Only check trains in same row as Philip
                    for char, positions in train_rows[train_row].items():
                        for train_col in positions:
                            # After 'turn' turns, train moves 2 cells left
                            current_col = train_col - 2 * turn
                            # If train is still in bounds and at same column
                            if current_col >= 0 and current_col == new_col:
                                collision = True
                                break
                        if collision:
                            break
                    if collision:
                        break
                        
            if not collision:
                state = (new_row, new_col, turn + 1)
                if state not in visited:
                    visited.add(state)
                    queue.append(state)
                    
    return "NO"

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

from collections import deque

def solve():
    n, k = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    for _ in range(3):
        grid.append(input().strip())
    
    # Store trains by row: row -> {char: list of column positions}
    train_rows = [dict() for _ in range(3)]
    
    # Find Philip's initial position
    philip_row, philip_col = 0, 0
    
    for i in range(3):
        for j in range(n):
            if grid[i][j] == 's':
                philip_row, philip_col = i, j
            elif grid[i][j] != '.':
                char = grid[i][j]
                if char not in train_rows[i]:
                    train_rows[i][char] = []
                train_rows[i][char].append(j)
    
    # BFS: (row, col, turn)
    queue = deque([(philip_row, philip_col, 0)])
    visited = set()
    visited.add((philip_row, philip_col, 0))
    
    # Directions: up, down, stay
    while queue:
        row, col, turn = queue.popleft()
        
        # Move right to col+1
        new_col = col + 1
        
        # If we've reached the rightmost column
        if new_col >= n:
            return "YES"
            
        # Try vertical moves: up, down, stay
        for dr in [-1, 0, 1]:
            new_row = row + dr
            if new_row < 0 or new_row >= 3:
                continue
                
            # Check collision with trains after 'turn' turns
            collision = False
            for train_row in range(3):
                if train_row == new_row:  # Only check trains in same row as Philip
                    for char, positions in train_rows[train_row].items():
                        for train_col in positions:
                            # After 'turn' turns, train moves 2 cells left
                            current_col = train_col - 2 * turn
                            # If train is still in bounds and at same column
                            if current_col >= 0 and current_col == new_col:
                                collision = True
                                break
                        if collision:
                            break
                    if collision:
                        break
                        
            if not collision:
                state = (new_row, new_col, turn + 1)
                if state not in visited:
                    visited.add(state)
                    queue.append(state)
                    
    return "NO"

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
